So starfix could hold, for a principal, the identity the authority puts in its certificates, write a principals file beside or in place of the keys file, and hold no key at all for such a person. Joining is one `sfx admin` command, or none if membership follows a group; a new laptop is nothing, since the person signs in again; leaving is the authority no longer issuing, within hours, and `principal disable` at once. Expiry and the second factor are the authority's.

Not tried, and needed before a design: that `sfx`'s in-process SSH client offers a certificate (it takes its signers from ssh-agent, which can hold certificates; a `key:` file would need its `-cert.pub` read beside it); a real authority; what replaces the key's fingerprint on a connection (the certificate's serial or key id), so that revoking and dropping still work; and groups in a certificate mapped to memberships.

**Other routes.** Provisioning principals and memberships from a directory (SCIM, or a job that reads the directory and runs `sfx admin`) is complementary: it answers who is a member, not how they prove who they are. Signing in to starfix itself with OpenID Connect needs an HTTPS transport, which decision 9 defers.

**Hiding who a person is.** Three depths, each a different amount of work:

1. **In telemetry only:** the stand-ins of §16.4. Designed there.
2. **A name that is not a person's name.** Nothing stops an operator naming principals `u-7f3a2c` today, but then every list shows only that. A display name and an external identity (the single sign-on subject) on the principal's row in the control store, shown by `sfx` to those who may list principals, would let the name in every project's log be an opaque handle while people still see who is who. Forgetting a person is then deleting one row. This is a small change to the control store and the clients, and none to a project's database.
3. **Renaming, or erasing a name already written.** A project's event log is its history and Dolt keeps it; a principal's name is in it as text. Changing that means an id in place of the name throughout a project's schema, and a migration of every log. Large, and only worth it if 2 does not satisfy a legal requirement.

**What would have to be chosen:** whether certificates become a second way in beside keys or the way for servers above some size; whether membership may follow a group; and which of the depths above is wanted. Until then, keys and names work as §15.2 and §15.3 describe.

## 16. Observability

Designed 12 Oct 2026. Slices O1, O2 and O3 of §16.8 are built, and the **As built** notes at the end of this section say how; so is slice O4, whose note is at the end of §16.5; slice O5, the harness, is not. When this was written starfixd had structured logs (text or JSON) on stderr, `sfx admin status`, and nothing else: no metrics a monitor can collect, no traces, no health check, and no way to read status on the server without an admin's SSH key.

**Goal.** An operator can wire starfixd, and the Dolt behind it, into whatever they already run, on any cloud or none, by configuration alone. Telemetry must never slow, block or fail a request.

### 16.1 Principle

starfixd speaks open formats on the machine it runs on, and **a collector on the same machine is the vendor adapter**. The OpenTelemetry Collector, and every vendor's and cloud's own agent, read these formats and forward to their backend. Nothing in starfixd names a vendor, and starfixd sends nothing anywhere unless it is configured to.

| Signal | From starfixd | From Dolt |
|---|---|---|
| Metrics | OTLP push to a collector, and the same instruments on a Prometheus endpoint for pull | Its own Prometheus endpoint (§16.5) |
| Traces | OTLP push | None: Dolt has no tracing |
| Logs | JSON lines on stderr, to the journal, as now, with a stable schema; OTLP push as an option | JSON lines, to the journal |
| Health | `/healthz` and `/readyz` on the loopback listener; systemd readiness and watchdog | The unit's state; its metrics endpoint answering |

Both pull and push are offered because operators differ: a Prometheus shop scrapes and runs no collector, an OTLP shop does not scrape, and Dolt can only be scraped, so most servers will have something that scrapes anyway.

### 16.2 The SDK, and what it costs

Measured 12 Oct 2026 with the release build flags (`CGO_ENABLED=0 -trimpath -ldflags "-s -w"`), in a program of a few lines, against a baseline of `log/slog` and `net/http`:

| Linked | Binary | Third-party modules |
|---|---|---|
| Baseline | 6.3 MB | 0 |
| SDK, traces and metrics, OTLP over HTTP (`otel` v1.47.0) | +9.3 MB | 24, among them `google.golang.org/grpc` and `google.golang.org/protobuf` |
| The above, the SDK's Prometheus exporter, OTLP logs and the `slog` bridge | +10.6 MB | 34, adding `prometheus/client_golang` and its own |

starfixd is 10.1 MB today with about 11 modules, so it roughly doubles. That is against rule 4's grain, and decision 28 accepts it for starfixd. To keep the cost where it was accepted:

- **`sfx` links none of it.** The client is on every developer's machine and inside every agent's harness. It passes trace context on (decision 27) and starts no span. A test fails if `cmd/sfx`, or any package it imports, imports `go.opentelemetry.io`.
- **One package owns it.** `internal/telemetry` is the only importer of the SDK and exporters. The server, the store and the commands see a small interface of starfix's own (start a span, record a measurement), so the SDK can be replaced, or compiled to nothing for a test, in one place.
- **OTLP over HTTP only.** No gRPC exporter: it would add more of gRPC than the HTTP exporter already drags in.
- **Each module added is named, with its reason, in the pull request** that adds it (rule 4), and the weekly `govulncheck` covers them.
- **The logs exporter is experimental** upstream (the Logs API and SDK reached release candidate in 2026; the exporters are outside that). So OTLP logs are an option, off by default, and the journal stays the logs' home.

### 16.3 Configuration

- **Off by default.** With nothing configured starfixd exports nothing, listens on nothing new and behaves as now.
- **The standard `OTEL_*` environment variables** configure export, as for any OpenTelemetry program, so an operator's existing knowledge and tooling apply: `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` (a collector on the machine, `http://127.0.0.1:4318`, is the documented setup), `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` (default `starfixd`), `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES`, `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER`, `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED`. The resource carries `service.name`, `service.version` and `service.instance.id`.
- **`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` can hold a token** for a hosted backend. It is a secret (rule 2): it comes from the unit's `EnvironmentFile`, mode 0600, never from argv, and starfixd never logs it. Sending to a collector on the machine needs none, which is one more reason to prefer that.
- **`telemetry:` in starfixd's settings** holds what is starfix's own: `listen` (the loopback listener, below), `per_project` (on by default), `principals` (how a person appears, §16.4), `audit_log` (on by default), `dolt_metrics_url` (§16.5).
- **The listener** (decision 29): `telemetry.listen: 127.0.0.1:9464`, say. An address that is not loopback is refused at start with a fix. It serves `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/readyz` and nothing else, with a cap on requests in flight and a deadline on each (rule 18). It has no authentication: any user on the machine can read it, and it carries project ids, names and counts, and no secret, principal name or issue text. A failure to bind at start fails the start, with a fix, as a keys file that cannot be written does.

### 16.4 What is measured

**Metrics.** Named in OpenTelemetry's style; on the Prometheus endpoint the exporter writes them with underscores and unit suffixes.

| Instrument | Kind | Attributes |
|---|---|---|
| `starfix.request.duration` | histogram, seconds | `op`, `result` |
| `starfix.project.requests` | counter | `project`, `result_class` (ok, refused, error) |
| `starfix.connections` | up-down counter | `project` |
| `starfix.handshakes` | counter | `result` (the refusal's code, or ok) |
| `starfix.connections.dropped` | counter | `reason` (membership, disabled, key, archived, idle, limit) |
| `starfix.limit.refusals` | counter | `limit` |
| `starfix.store.write.duration` | histogram, seconds | `project` |
| `starfix.store.write.retries` | counter | `project` |
| `starfix.store.commit.duration` | histogram, seconds | none |
| `starfix.stores.open` | gauge | none |
| `starfix.store.open.duration` | histogram, seconds | `migrated` |
| `starfix.claims` | counter | `event` (take, renew, release, expire), `reason` |
| `starfix.control.writes` | counter | `op`, `result` |
| `starfix.keys_file.writes` | counter | `result` |
| `starfix.keys_file.last_write.time` | gauge, unix seconds | none |
| `starfix.reaper.duration`, `starfix.reaper.last_run.time` | histogram, gauge | none |
| `starfix.telemetry.dropped` | counter | `signal` |
| Go runtime and process | from `runtime/metrics` | none |

- **Bounded (rule 18).** An attribute's values come from a fixed list in the code: ops, codes, reasons, limits. An op the server does not know is `unknown`. A principal, session, machine, issue id or anything else a client chooses is never an attribute of a metric; those are for traces and logs.
- **`project` is on few instruments, and never beside `op`.** 500 projects by 60 ops by 10 results by a histogram's buckets would be hundreds of thousands of series. So one instrument has `op` and `result`, and separate, coarser ones have `project`. `telemetry.per_project: false` drops the attribute everywhere, for a server near the project cap.
- **Histograms have a short fixed list of buckets,** 5 ms to 10 s, the same everywhere.

**Traces.**
- One server span for each request, named for its op, with the op, result code, project and protocol version, and the principal and session as `telemetry.principals` allows (below). A trace may carry them; a metric never does. Its parent is the client's trace context when the request carries a valid one (decision 27).
- Child spans where time goes: the wait for the writer, each attempt of a write (a retry is a new one), the Dolt commit, a store's open and migration.
- A span for a handshake, and root spans for the reaper, the sweep, a keys file write and a control index reload, so background work is visible.
- No span carries an issue's title or body, a memory's text, a comment, a key or a DSN. Ids only.
- Sampling is the SDK's, by `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER`; the default follows the parent and otherwise samples everything, since a tracker's request volume is small.
- `sfx` reads `TRACEPARENT` and `TRACESTATE` from its environment and sends them, so a trace begun by an agent's harness continues into starfix. Per-call context from an MCP request's metadata is part of this stage.

**Logs.**
- The JSON schema becomes a documented contract: `time`, `level`, `msg`, and where they apply `event`, `project`, `principal`, `session`, `op`, `code`, `dur_ms`, `req`, `trace_id`, `span_id`.
- **`event` is a stable name** for anything worth an alert (`keys_file.write_failed`, `project.open_failed`, `conn.cap_refused`), so an alert matches a name, not a sentence that may be reworded.
- **`req`** is an id for each request, on every line about it and in the refusal the client sees, so a person's report finds the server's lines.
- **Audit stream** (decision 30): each event of the control store's log (a project added or archived, a principal added, disabled or enabled, a key added or removed, a membership set or removed) is also written as a log line with `audit=true`, the actor, the op and the target, after the write commits. Fingerprints, never keys. The event log stays the record; the line is a copy for a SIEM that reads the journal.
- With `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp` the same records also go out over OTLP, through the `slog` bridge. Stderr is never turned off.

**People in telemetry** (decision 32). `telemetry.principals` is one of:

| Value | A span or an exported log record carries |
|---|---|
| `pseudonym` (default) | A stable stand-in, `p_` and 16 hex characters: an HMAC-SHA-256 of the principal's name under a key that starfixd generates and keeps in a 0600 file. The same person is the same stand-in in every trace and line, so their requests can still be followed and counted; the backend never learns who. `sfx admin principal whois p_…`, for a server admin, gives the name. Deleting the key file makes every earlier stand-in unlinkable to anyone, which is what a retention rule may want. |
| `name` | The principal's name |
| `omit` | Nothing about who |

- It governs what leaves the machine through telemetry: spans, and log records exported over OTLP. Sessions are stand-ins or omitted with the principal, since a session id can name a person's machine.
- **It does not change the journal or the audit stream.** Those are the server's own records, an access change with no actor is not an audit record, and whoever can read the journal can read the databases. An operator who ships the journal to a third party filters it in the collector, and the guide shows how.
- A stand-in hides a name from the backend. It does not make a person anonymous: what they did, when and in which project is still there. The guide says so.

**The Go runtime's metrics** (decision 33). Measured 12 Oct 2026 on an Apple M4 Max with Go 1.27.1: reading the fifteen an operator wants (heap, GC, goroutines, scheduler latency, CPU, the memory limit) from the standard library's `runtime/metrics` takes 0.45 µs and allocates nothing; reading every metric the runtime has takes 1.6 µs. Done once for each export or scrape, that is nothing. The older `runtime.ReadMemStats` takes 22 µs and stops the world, and is not used. So the cost of the standard library is about sixty lines of code and names of our own choosing. The contrib module that does the same reading (`go.opentelemetry.io/contrib/instrumentation/runtime`) adds 86 KB and one module, and gives the names of OpenTelemetry's semantic conventions, which ready-made dashboards expect. starfixd uses the module, for the names; it is below version 1, so if it breaks, the sixty lines replace it.

**Health.**
- `/healthz` answers while the process serves. `/readyz` answers only when the daemon is listening, its last check of Dolt (a cheap query, cached a few seconds, never run per request) passed, and the keys file's last write did not fail; otherwise it says which, in one line.
- systemd: `Type=notify` with readiness sent once the daemon listens, and a watchdog fed by the reaper tick, so a daemon that hangs is restarted. On other systems neither is sent.
- `starfixd status [--json]`, run on the server as the daemon's user over its socket, prints what `sfx admin status` prints, for a script or an agent that has no admin key.

### 16.5 Dolt

Measured 12 Oct 2026 at Dolt 2.4.2. A `metrics:` block (`host`, `port`, `labels`) in Dolt's config turns on a Prometheus endpoint at `/metrics`, with no authentication, so it is bound to loopback:

| Group | Metrics |
|---|---|
| Server | `dss_concurrent_connections`, `dss_concurrent_queries`, `dss_connects`, `dss_disconnects`, `dss_query_duration` (histogram; buckets 0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100, 1000 s), `dss_dolt_version` |
| Process | `process_resident_memory_bytes`, `process_cpu_seconds_total`, `process_open_fds`, `process_max_fds`, `process_start_time_seconds` |
| Go runtime | `go_memstats_*`, `go_gc_*` (among them `go_gc_gomemlimit_bytes`), `go_goroutines` |
| System | `sys_cpu_usage`, `sys_mem_usage`, `sys_disk_usage` |

- **Nothing is per database.** Dolt cannot tell one project from another; starfixd's own per-project instruments (§16.4) are the only such view.
- **No tracing,** in its flags, its config or its documentation. A trace ends at starfixd's span around the SQL it ran.
- **Logs** are text or JSON (`log_format`), to the journal.
- Over SQL, starfixd's least-privileged account can read `SHOW STATUS`, the process list and a table's estimated rows and size. None of it is needed for what follows.

What starfix does with it:
- **The server guide and the Docker image turn Dolt's endpoint on,** on loopback, and the collector configuration scrapes it beside starfixd's. Dolt's metrics reach the backend under Dolt's own names; starfixd does not re-export them, which would count them twice.
- **`sfx admin status` shows Dolt's memory, connections and uptime** (decision 30), closing the gap §15.4 left. With `telemetry.dolt_metrics_url` set (loopback only), starfixd reads that endpoint on a timer, with a deadline and a size limit, parses the handful of lines it needs with a parser of its own, and shows when it last succeeded. A failure shows as "unknown", never as an error of starfixd's.
- **Dolt's memory limit.** `go_gc_gomemlimit_bytes` beside `process_resident_memory_bytes` is what tells an operator whether the `GOMEMLIMIT` of §15.5, 2 is set with room to spare. The alert rules include it. *Measured 12 Oct 2026 (§15.5, 2):* that pair separates a limit that is too low only roughly (resident memory was up to 4% over the limit where it cost little, and from 4.8% over where it cost nine times the CPU or more). The collector's cycles (`go_gc_duration_seconds_count`) against starfixd's writes separate it cleanly: under 3 for each 100 writes without trouble, more than 14 past the cliff.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O4: Dolt's own figures, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O4 of §16.8 is built: Dolt's endpoint on in the guide and the Docker image, `telemetry.dolt_metrics_url`, and Dolt's figures in `sfx admin status` and `starfixd status`. The collector configuration that scrapes Dolt and the alert rules are slice O5's and are not. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **The port is 11228**, which the guide chose: Dolt has no default (`port: -1` is off), 11228 is free on stock Ubuntu 24.04, below the range the kernel gives outgoing connections, and is neither Dolt's 3306, the 9464 the guide gives starfixd's listener, the 4318 of OTLP, nor one of the 909x ports a Prometheus installation uses. A test holds the guide, the image and the refusals' example to it.
- **"No authentication" is how the guide configures it, not all Dolt can do.** Dolt 2.4.2's `metrics:` block also takes `tls_cert`, `tls_key`, `tls_ca` and `jwt_required_for_localhost`, and its log says `auth_enabled = false` at start. None of that was tried. starfixd reads plain HTTP on loopback and refuses anything else, so a server that turned those on would show Dolt as unknown.
- **The URL** must be `http://` with a host, a port, a path beyond `/`, and no user, query or fragment: it is logged whole, so it has no place for a secret. An IP address that is not loopback is refused when the settings are read, by every command; a name is resolved when the daemon starts and refused unless every address is loopback. Each connection is then checked again against the address it reaches (the dialer's `Control`), so a name that changes later leads nowhere else. `STARFIXD_TELEMETRY_DOLT_METRICS_URL` sets it too. `telemetry.CheckAddr` was not reused: its refusals name `listen:`.
- **The reader** is `internal/doltmetrics`, on the standard library alone. One GET every 15 seconds from a goroutine `Serve` owns, started at once and stopped with it; 5 seconds for the whole read; no redirect followed, no proxy, no compression asked for, no connection kept. The parser reads at most 1 MiB, passes over a line longer than 4,096 bytes, and looks only at six series by name; a series given more than once is believed in neither line, and a value that is not a finite number in the figure's range is left out. A body with none of the six is a failed read. It has a fuzz target. Dolt 2.4.2 serves 10 KiB in lines of at most 250 bytes.
- **The figures, and why each:** the version (`dss_dolt_version`, which packs major, minor and patch into one number), uptime (`process_start_time_seconds`), connections (`dss_concurrent_connections`), resident memory (`process_resident_memory_bytes`), the memory limit (`go_gc_gomemlimit_bytes`, which is the largest int64 with none set) and the collector's cycles (`go_gc_duration_seconds_count`). Operator labels (`labels:`) attach to the `dss_` series only, and are read past. **Left out:** query latency, since `dss_query_duration`'s buckets start at 10 ms and step by tens, which says less than the report's own P50 and P99 of each project's writes; `go_memstats_next_gc_bytes`, which the measurements found did not separate a good limit from a bad one; and Dolt's CPU, which a collector has from Dolt directly.
- **Cycles for each 100 writes** is the one figure that exists nowhere else, so starfixd derives it: the difference in Dolt's cycle count against the difference in its own count of writes, between the latest read and the latest one at least ten minutes older, or the oldest it has. The writes are the project stores' (`Store.Writes`, counted as `WRITES/MIN` is), summed at each read over the stores then open; a store that closes between two reads takes with it what it wrote since the earlier one, and the control database's writes are not counted. A change of Dolt's start time, or a cycle count that went back, starts the window again. The ratio is printed from 500 writes in the window, because an idle Dolt still collects about every two minutes (measured: 3 cycles in the first 30 seconds, then one every two minutes).
- **The verdict** is the client's (`internal/statustext`), from the measurements' two numbers: under 3 with room, over 14 too low, and between them said to be between. It is given only when a limit is set. One rule was added after the run on the virtual machine below: with fewer than 500 writes, cycles past 14 for each 100 of 500 writes (more than 70 in the window) are judged too low all the same, because a Dolt under a limit of under half its resident memory made 500 cycles in 15 seconds with nothing written, and "too few writes to judge" would have been false. The status line and the guide say the thresholds come from one machine and one shape of load.
- **Unknown.** A read that fails for any reason leaves the report saying `Dolt: unknown` with the time of the last read that worked, and no figure: stale memory figures were judged worse than none. A read that works but lacks a series shows that figure alone as unknown. A failure is logged at warn when it starts, every ten minutes while it lasts with the count, and at info when it ends; it changes neither `/readyz`, which keeps its own ping of Dolt, nor any request.
- **No instrument was added** for the derived figure. `Telemetry.Watch` has no way to say "unknown", so a gauge would hold a last value through a failure, or a zero; and wherever both starfixd and Dolt are scraped, which is the documented setup, the backend can divide `go_gc_duration_seconds_count` by the sum of `starfix_store_write_duration_seconds_count` itself. A server that pushes straight to a hosted backend with no collector has nothing of Dolt there, and has the status report.
- **Result fields only.** `ServerStatus.dolt` joins protocol 5, which no release speaks; an older client ignores it.
- **Found on the way, and left:** while a project's store is open, the status report makes one query for that store's sessions, so with Dolt frozen whole (`SIGSTOP`) `starfixd status` waited and gave up after its 30 seconds instead of saying `Dolt: unknown`. `/readyz` said `the last check of Dolt failed`. Bounding that query is not this slice's.
- **Run on the virtual machine of §15's notes** (Ubuntu 24.04.5 on arm64, the three AppArmor profiles enforced), set up by the guide's own block for a server set up before. The endpoint answered on 127.0.0.1 and was refused on the machine's other address. The report's memory was within a mebibyte of `VmRSS`, its start time `ps`'s, its connections the two `ss` showed starfixd holding. Over a burst the collector line gave the cycles and writes that Dolt's counter and `starfix_store_write_duration_seconds_count` gave: 4 cycles for 342 writes with no limit. Under `GOMEMLIMIT=2GiB`: 7 cycles for 562 writes, 1.2 for each 100, 4.2 ms of Dolt's CPU a write. Under `GOMEMLIMIT=120MiB`, with Dolt at 265 MiB at rest: 3,441 for 562, 612 for each 100, 55 ms a write, and a median write of 20 ms against 5 to 8. With the port refused for 50 seconds: `Dolt: unknown` with the last read, `/readyz` 200, one warning, one line when it ended. A second `telemetry:` key in the config file stops the daemon (`mapping key "telemetry" already defined`), which the guide warns of. With `host: 0.0.0.0` Dolt listened on every address; Dolt's unit (`IPAddressAllow=localhost`) left a request to the other address, made on the machine, unanswered. starfixd's unit held to loopback the same way still read. No rule was added to an AppArmor profile, and the kernel log had no denial. The 76 checks passed, after their script was changed for decisions 35, 36 and 38.
- **Not run:** amd64; a real collector scraping Dolt beside starfixd; a request to Dolt's metrics from another machine; Dolt's TLS and token settings; the thresholds against real traffic or days of uptime, which is the measurements' own gap.

### 16.6 Robustness

- **A request never waits for telemetry.** Recording a measurement or ending a span is memory only. Export happens on the SDK's own goroutines, in batches, with bounded queues and a timeout; a full queue drops, and `starfix.telemetry.dropped` counts it.
- **A collector that is down, slow or refusing changes nothing** but the drop counter and one sampled warning. A test points the exporter at a closed port and at a server that never answers, runs the soak's mix of work, and checks latency, goroutines and memory against a run with telemetry off.
- **Export errors go to starfixd's log, sampled,** through the SDK's error handler, never to stderr unformatted and never once a second forever.
- **Stopping flushes with a deadline,** so a stop is not held up by a dead collector.
- **Cardinality is bounded by construction** (§16.4), and a test fails if an instrument gains an attribute that is not on the list.

### 16.7 The harness

- **`deploy/observability/`**: an OpenTelemetry Collector configuration (OTLP in; Prometheus scrapes of starfixd and Dolt; out to whatever the operator names), alert rules in Prometheus's rule format, which most systems read or translate, and one dashboard. They are examples that are tested, not a product.
- **A Compose profile** beside the Docker test server runs a Collector, pinned by digest, wired to starfixd and Dolt and writing what it receives to a file. The smoke test, asked for it, then checks that each expected metric arrived from both, that a trace arrived whose root is the trace id the test put in `TRACEPARENT` with the server's spans beneath it, that an access change produced its audit line, and that `/readyz` turns unready when Dolt is stopped. A second profile adds a metrics store, a trace store and the dashboard, for a person to look at.
- **The soak checks the numbers.** It already replays every project's event log. At the end it reads the instruments in process and asserts that they say what the log says: requests by result, claims taken, released by reason and expired, connections dropped by reason, control writes. A metric that is present and wrong fails the gate.
- **Alerts shipped:** starfixd not ready; a keys file write failing; an active project that cannot be opened; the reaper not having run; refusals at a connection or store cap; write latency; Dolt's memory against its limit and against the machine's; disk.

### 16.8 Plan

| Slice | Delivers |
|---|---|
| O1 | `internal/telemetry`, the SDK and OTLP export of metrics, the settings, the loopback listener with `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/readyz`, systemd readiness and watchdog, `starfixd status`, the instruments of §16.4, and the test that keeps the SDK out of `sfx` |
| O2 | Traces: the spans of §16.4 from the context decision 27 carries, per-call context in `sfx mcp`, and trace ids in the logs |
| O3 | The log schema, `event` names, `req`, the audit stream, and OTLP logs as an option |
| O4 | Dolt: its endpoint on in the guide and the image, and its figures in `sfx admin status` |
| O5 | The harness: Collector configuration, Compose profiles, smoke checks, alert rules, dashboard, the soak's cross-check, and the tests of §16.6 |

### 16.9 Settled since the first draft

1. **Push and pull for metrics** (decision 31): both, as §16.1 has it.
2. **People in traces** (decision 32): the `telemetry.principals` setting of §16.4, defaulting to a pseudonym.
3. **Go runtime metrics** (decision 33): the contrib module, after measuring the standard library (§16.4).
4. **A token for a hosted backend** (decision 34): allowed, from a 0600 environment file. The guide's first setup sends to a collector on the machine, which needs none and keeps the secret, the retries and the vendor's address out of starfixd; sending straight to a hosted backend is the second, for a server where running a collector is not wanted.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O1: metrics, the listener, health and `starfixd status`, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O1 of §16.8 is built: `internal/telemetry`, the SDK for metrics with OTLP/HTTP push and the Prometheus exporter, the `telemetry:` settings `listen` and `per_project`, the loopback listener with `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/readyz`, systemd readiness and watchdog, `starfixd status`, the instruments of §16.4 and the tests that keep the SDK in its one package and out of `sfx`. Traces, the log schema, `event`, `req` and the audit stream, Dolt's figures, `telemetry.principals`, `audit_log` and `dolt_metrics_url`, and the harness of §16.7 are not built; a config file that sets one of those three keys is refused as holding an unknown key. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **What it cost.** With the release flags, `starfixd` went from 10.8 MB to 20.6 MB for linux/amd64 and from 10.1 MB to 19.7 MB for darwin/arm64, and from 4 third-party modules linked to 35 (34 off Linux), counting the modules whose packages `go list -deps ./cmd/starfixd` names; §16.2's "about 11" was a looser count. `sfx` is the same size to the byte and links the same 12 third-party modules. Two of those 12 rose a version, `golang.org/x/oauth2` from v0.35.0 to v0.37.0 and `golang.org/x/sync` from v0.20.0 to v0.23.0: the module has one build list, and `github.com/prometheus/common` and `golang.org/x/text`, which only `starfixd` links, require the newer ones.
- **The variables are read by starfix, not left to the SDK.** `internal/telemetry` takes the environment's lookup from the main package (rule 15) and parses the endpoint, headers, protocol, timeouts, interval, exporter, service name, resource attributes and `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED` itself, so that a test can give it an environment and a bad value is refused at start with a fix. The SDK still reads from the process environment, on its own, what starfix does not parse: the TLS files and compression of the OTLP exporter. `OTEL_TRACES_*` and `OTEL_LOGS_*` do nothing yet.
- **No retry within an interval.** The exporter's own retry is off: the totals are cumulative, so the next export carries what a failed one did, and a retry only holds the exporter's goroutine for longer. A failed export is handled in one place, a wrapper around the exporter: it counts on `starfix.telemetry.dropped`, logs through a sampler of its own (the first failure, then one line every 10 minutes with the number left out, and a line when exports succeed again) and returns nothing to the SDK, whose handler would log it again each interval. The server's refusal sampler did not fit: it is per principal and per minute, and the server imports this package, not the other way round. `starfix.telemetry.dropped` counts failed exports, not data points, and its `signal` is only `metrics` so far.
- **The SDK's own errors** (its process-wide error handler and internal logger) go to the same sampler, redacted, in place of stderr. Both are settings of the process, so a process makes one exporting `Telemetry`.
- **A token cannot reach the log.** Every header's value, and the password and query of the endpoint, are removed from each line before it is written, because the exporter's error does repeat the body of a backend's refusal. The start-up line gives the number of headers, not their names.
- **Recording and locks.** A measurement takes the SDK's aggregation lock for its instrument, which a collection holds only while it copies the numbers; the periodic reader exports after the collection has returned, and the Prometheus handler writes its response after gathering. `internal/telemetry` adds one lock of its own, a read lock on the set of projects seen, held for a map lookup.
- **Attribute values are enforced in `internal/telemetry`,** not left to its callers: each attribute has its list there, the server gives it the list of ops, and a value on no list is recorded as `unknown` (an op) or `other`. `project` must have a UUID's shape and be one of at most as many as the `projects` limit.
- **Instruments, where the code has what the table did not, or lacks it:**
  - `starfix.handshakes` has the result `failed` for a handshake that ended with no protocol error to name: a first frame that could not be read or was not a bridge frame, a welcome that could not be written. A connection refused before its handshake (a peer that is not the daemon's user, a socket past twice `conns`) is not counted as one.
  - `starfix.connections.dropped` has the reasons `membership`, `disabled`, `archived`, `key`, `idle`, `limit` and one more, `access`: a connection whose access ended while its handshake ran. `limit` is a socket closed unread past twice `conns`; a refusal at `conns` or `conns_per_principal` is a handshake answered `busy`, not a dropped connection, and is counted on `starfix.handshakes` and `starfix.limit.refusals`. The admin's own connection, closed after the reply to the change that ended its access, is counted with the others.
  - `starfix.limit.refusals` names the limits the server enforces itself and the store's caps that have an error type of their own: `conns`, `conns_per_principal`, `handshake_sockets`, `write_rate`, `control_write_rate`, `open_stores`, `telemetry_requests`, `projects`, `principals`, `keys_per_principal`, `members_per_project`, `prices`, `plans`, `hours_per_day`, `usage_per_day`. The store refuses a request past its other limits (labels, dependencies, acceptance items, memories and the rest) as plain invalid, which nothing tells apart from any other invalid request, so those are not counted by name.
  - `starfix.claims` is counted from the store, after each write commits, so it says what the event log says: `take`, `release` and `expire` are the events `claim.take`, `claim.release` and `claim.expire`. `reason` is `membership`, `disabled` or `archived` for a release whose event gives that reason (decision 21) and `none` for everything else: the store does not record whether a release came from a finish, a releasing handoff, a close or an import, and a take that replaces a lapsed holder is one `take`, not also an end. `renew` is not an event; it counts each lease a renew extended, which a renew with most of its lease left does not. A session that starts an issue it already holds extends its lease too, and is counted as nothing.
  - `starfix.store.write.duration` is the measurement behind the status report's P50 and P99, taken once: a write that changed a row and committed. `starfix.store.commit.duration` covers the project stores' Dolt commits, failed ones included, and not the control database's.
  - `starfix.control.writes` counts each `sfx admin` change asked for, by its result, refusals and rate-limited requests included.
  - `starfix.stores.open` counts stores that are open, opening or closing, as the status report does.
  - `starfix.keys_file.writes` has the results `ok` and `failed`; a tick that finds the file already right is not a write.
  - **Go runtime, without the process.** The contrib module at v0.72.0 gives `go.memory.used`, `go.memory.allocated`, `go.memory.allocations`, `go.memory.gc.goal`, `go.memory.limit`, `go.goroutine.count`, `go.processor.limit` and `go.config.gogc`. It has no GC pause or CPU metric. Its scheduler latency comes only as a producer that reports the runtime's own histogram, with far more buckets than the short list above and outside any view, so it is left out. No process metrics (resident memory, file descriptors) are exported.
- **The Prometheus endpoint** leaves the scope labels off each series (`otel_scope_name`), and carries the resource in `target_info`.
- **The store's hook** is `store.Options.Observer`, an interface of four methods (a write that committed, a retry, a Dolt commit, a change of a claim), called after the work and outside any transaction. With it came `Store.Migrated` (for `migrated`) and `Store.Ping` (for `/readyz`). The store imports nothing of telemetry.
- **`/readyz`.** The check of Dolt is a ping on a pooled connection to the control database, or to the one project's, with a deadline of 2 seconds. It is made when `/readyz` is asked and the last answer is 5 seconds old, never more often, and requests that arrive together share one check; nothing runs on a timer. A project that could not be opened at start does not make the server unready: `starfixd upgrade`'s health check is what holds that. One more line was added, `starfixd is starting`: the listener is opened before the database, so a monitor can watch a start that migrates many projects.
- **The listener** resolves a host name at start and binds the address it checked, not the name. Beside the cap on requests in flight (`telemetry_requests`, 8) it holds at most twice that many connections open and closes one more unanswered, since a cap on requests alone does not stop a local user from holding file descriptors with idle connections. `STARFIXD_TELEMETRY_LISTEN` sets the address as well as the config file. With `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` the listener still answers health, and `/metrics` is 404.
- **What the SDK links, and what the listener serves.** The OTLP/HTTP exporter links `google.golang.org/grpc` (66 packages: the exporter's configuration code and the generated code of the OTLP protocol import it, and no gRPC connection is made) and with it `golang.org/x/net/trace`, which registers `/debug/requests` and `/debug/events` on Go's default HTTP mux, as `expvar` does `/debug/vars`. The listener serves a mux of its own with three paths, never the default one, and a test asks for those pages and gets 404. `govulncheck` still counts `html/template`, which `x/net/trace` uses, as reachable through `http.Server.Serve`: two standard-library findings more than before this slice (GO-2026-6599 and GO-2026-6600, both fixed in Go 1.27.2). `golang.org/x/net` is at v0.60.0, not the v0.59.0 the SDK asks for, which had five findings of its own.
- **The watchdog is fed by a ticker in the reaper's goroutine,** at half `WATCHDOG_USEC`, not by the reaper's tick itself, which comes every 30 seconds whatever the unit asks. A reaper that hangs, or one tick that outlasts `WatchdogSec`, stops the feeding. The guide's unit has `Type=notify`, `NotifyAccess=main`, `WatchdogSec=120` and `TimeoutStartSec=600`: with `Type=notify` systemd waits for the daemon to say it is ready, by default for 90 seconds, and a start that migrates many projects can take longer. The notifications are tested against a socket standing in for systemd's, on Linux; the unit itself was not run under a real systemd for this note, and was afterward (the next note).
- **`starfixd status`** connects as the upgrade's probe does, under a second reserved principal, `starfixd-status`. Its connection is in no project, holds no connection slot and is answered only the status report. It asks for every page, so it prints every project. It also works on a server of one project, where `sfx admin status` is refused: it reports the server and that project, listed by its id, since only a control database names projects. The report's text moved to `internal/statustext`, which `sfx admin status` and `starfixd status` both print with.
- **Left for the later slices, and found on the way:** the `go-sql-driver/mysql` driver logs through its own logger, unformatted, to stderr when a pooled connection to Dolt dies (`closing bad idle connection`); slice O3's log schema will want that routed through starfixd's logger. The dead-collector test of §16.6 is written, at the server's level, against a closed port and a listener that never answers; the soak's cross-check and the Collector harness of §16.7 are not.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O1 merged with stage 3a's last changes and run under systemd, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O1 was written beside decisions 24 to 27 and the AppArmor profiles, and then merged with them and run on the Ubuntu 24.04 virtual machine of §15's notes (arm64, systemd 255, AppArmor 4.0.1, the three profiles enforced). What the merge and the machine settled:

- **`starfixd status` and the one protocol version (decision 25).** The upgrade's probe is welcomed whatever protocol it speaks, because it sends no request and the binary that runs it is the old one. `starfixd status` sends a request and reads a result, so it is held to the protocol like any client: one from another version is refused with `version`. Both names stay reserved, the bridge refuses both to every key, and every other reserved name is refused before the hello.
- **Trace context (decision 27)** rides the request's context from before the dispatch, and `observeRequest` measures the same stretch after it. A comment marks where slice O2 starts its span from `TraceFrom`. No span is built.
- **Claims ended by an archive (decision 24) are counted once,** as a release with the reason `archived`, whether the store was open, was opened for the archive, or was opened as it is at the daemon's start (`ReleaseArchived`, which now measures the stores it opens). A store opened at the start without migrating is opened beside the registry and adds nothing to `starfix.store.open.duration`.
- **The openings a daemon makes before it listens are measured now.** `OpenProjects` is where an upgrade's migrations run, and the first build of this slice measured only the openings a connection causes, so `migrated="true"` would hardly ever have been seen.
- **The archive lock.** `Server.archiving` is held across the control store's archive write and nothing else. What this slice added takes no lock while it holds another of the server's: the check of Dolt holds its own mutex for a ping, the count of open stores is read by the SDK's collection through the registry's mutex, and every recording is made after the server's or the registry's mutex is released, or under the keys file's or the connection counter's, which no collection takes.
- **An export cut short by the stop is not a failure.** The reader cancels an export that is under way when the daemon stops, and the first build logged and counted that as a collector's failure. It is now neither: the last export follows it.
- **The status report's text** carries the keys held back (decision 26), with its tests, in `internal/statustext`.
- **Under systemd.** `Type=notify`: `Started starfixd.service` is logged within a millisecond of `starfixd serving`, and systemd's debug log shows `READY=1`, `WATCHDOG=1` a minute later and `STOPPING=1` at the stop. `WatchdogSec=120`: a healthy daemon was left alone for 130 seconds; a frozen one (`SIGSTOP`) was killed with `SIGABRT` 120 seconds after its last notification, left its goroutines' stacks in the journal, and was started again. `TimeoutStartSec`: a start that never said it was ready was ended when the time ran out (run with 20 seconds). `systemd-analyze verify` accepts the unit. The 76 checks of §15's run passed again with all of it on, across a cold boot, with nothing in the kernel log.
- **The race with Dolt at boot is not removed by `Type=notify`**, since it is Dolt's unit that systemd counts as started before it listens. The unit's state is now true through it: `activating` until the start that serves. The same race follows every restart of Dolt's unit, a crash included, because `Requires=` has systemd stop starfixd with Dolt and start the two together; so `/readyz` reports a Dolt that hangs or cannot be reached, and a Dolt that restarts takes the listener down with starfixd for about five seconds. Having starfixd wait for Dolt at its start, now that the unit says when it is ready, would remove the failed start; it is not built.
- **AppArmor.** `starfixd-serve` gained one rule, write to `/run/systemd/notify`: without it the daemon serves and the unit never counts as started. The listener and a collector on the machine needed nothing new. A collector whose name needs DNS, and HTTPS, need three lines the shipped profile leaves out and its comments, its README and the guide give for `local/starfixd-serve`; each was shown to be needed and to be enough against receivers on the machine. `starfixd status` runs under the bridge's profile, by the binary's path, since it needs only the config file and the socket; root, to whom that profile gives no capability, runs it as the `starfix` user, and the refusal says so.
- **Memory.** Idle, a minute after its start, starfixd's resident memory was 13.9 MB before this slice, 20.3 MB with it and nothing configured, and 22.1 MB with metrics pushed and scraped (22.2 MB five minutes later). Of the 20.3 MB, 14.3 MB is the binary mapped from its file, against 8.5 MB before; the memory that is the process's own went from 5.4 MB to 6.0 MB, and to 6.4 MB with metrics on.
- **govulncheck** with Go 1.27.2, which the repository now pins, reports nothing: the standard-library findings of the note above, the two this slice had made reachable among them, are gone.
- **Still not run:** amd64; a real OpenTelemetry Collector or hosted backend (a few lines of Python received the pushes, over HTTP and over TLS with a private authority); the unit's start timeout at its real ten minutes; a migration long enough to need it.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O2: traces, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O2 of §16.8 is built: a tracer in `internal/telemetry`, exported with OTLP over HTTP; the spans of §16.4, from the caller's trace context (decision 27) through the request into the store; `telemetry.principals` with the stand-in key and `sfx admin principal whois` (decision 32); a trace context for each tool call of `sfx mcp`; and the ids of the server's span on its log lines. The log schema, `event`, `req`, the audit stream and OTLP logs (O3), Dolt's figures (O4) and the harness (O5) are not built. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **What it cost.** Two modules, the ones decision 28 approved for traces: `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/exporters/otlp/otlptrace` and `.../otlptrace/otlptracehttp`, both v1.47.0; the tracer itself, `sdk/trace`, is a package of a module `starfixd` already linked, and no other module's version changed. With the release flags, and measured against main after slice O4 merged, `starfixd` went from 20,824,224 to 21,414,048 bytes for linux/amd64 (2.8% more) and from 19,910,642 to 20,439,602 for darwin/arm64, and from 35 third-party modules linked to 37. `sfx` links the same 12, and none of the SDK: built from this tree with only the packages its own new code is in taken back to main's (the `whois` command, the per-call context, the new op, the status line), it is the same file as main's, byte for byte, on linux/amd64 and darwin/arm64. Its own new code adds 12,288 bytes on linux/amd64.
- **A span is made in one package, which decides what it may carry.** The server, the store's hook and the commands see `telemetry.Span`, a type of starfix's own. Its name is an op the server knows or one of eleven fixed names; its attributes are ten fixed keys, each with a rule for its values; its status is a code. It takes no error, since an error's message quotes what it refused, and it has no events and no links. Anything else it is given is dropped or recorded as `unknown` or `other`. So "no span carries an issue's title" is not a habit of the callers': a test sends every op with marked text in every string of its arguments, through a real server and store, and searches the spans in process and the bytes posted to a collector.
- **The caller's `tracestate` is not put on a span.** Decision 27 has the request carry it, and it does. The SDK would copy it from the remote parent onto every span, and export it; it is the caller's text, and the server calls nothing it could pass it on to. A span takes the trace id, the parent's span id and the sampled flag.
- **The store's hook is a second, smaller interface,** `store.Tracer`, beside `Observer`: one method, `Begin(ctx, stage, attempt)`, which returns the context for the stage's work and a func the store calls with how the stage ended (`ok`, `retry` or `error`; never the error). The stages are an attempt of a write, the wait within it, a Dolt commit and the migrations of an open. `Observer` is unchanged, and so are the figures of `sfx admin status` and the O1 metrics. The store still imports nothing of telemetry.
- **The wait for the writer** is the span around the start of the write's transaction on the store's one writer connection: the wait for the connection, and the round trip that begins the transaction, which cannot be told apart without changing how a write takes its connection. It is inside the attempt's span, since each attempt waits again.
- **A read has one span, the request's.** No span is made for each SQL statement: §16.5's "a trace ends at starfixd's span around the SQL it ran" is the request's span for a read, and the attempt's for a write.
- **Background spans are one trace for each tick,** not a root each as §16.4 has it: the reaper's span is the root, and the clock check, the reread of the control store, the keys file's write, the access sweep and the store's writes are beneath it, so that a slow tick shows which part was slow. Each is a root when it runs on its own, and the keys file's write after an `sfx admin` change is beneath that request's span. The clock check is one more than §16.4 lists (decision 37 came after it).
- **A handshake's span** covers the handshake and the note of the session in the registry that follows it, and has the opening of the project's store beneath it when this connection caused it. It is a root: a hello brings no trace context. `store.open` is a span only on a server of many projects, where the server opens stores; the one store of a server of one project is opened by `starfixd serve` before there is anything to parent it, and shows as `store.migrate` alone.
- **Failed means the server's failure.** A request answered `unavailable` marks its span failed, as OpenTelemetry's conventions have a server mark only its own errors; a refusal is the span's `result` and no failure. An attempt that lost to another transaction has the outcome `retry`; one that ended in any other error has `error`, which is most often the request's own refusal, so an attempt is never marked failed, and a commit, a migration or a wait that errs is.
- **People.** The stand-in is `p_` and the first 16 hex digits of HMAC-SHA-256 of the principal's name under a key of 32 random bytes; a session's is `s_` and the same of the session id, with a prefix inside the HMAC so that a session named for a principal is not that principal's stand-in. With `name`, a session id that is not 1 to 255 printable ASCII characters with no space is left out, since it is the client's text. Only a request's and a handshake's span say who.
- **Where the key lives.** `telemetry: principals_key:`, by default `telemetry/principals.key` under the home directory of the user starfixd runs as: `/var/lib/starfix/telemetry/principals.key`. A directory of its own, because the unit's `ProtectSystem=strict` leaves the home read-only and `ReadWritePaths` works on directories: the only other writable one, `.ssh`, is sshd's, is not writable on a server of one project, and holds `authorized_keys`, which the daemon must never be able to write. `StateDirectory=` would have made the whole home writable. The operator makes the directory and names it in a drop-in, as for the keys file; the AppArmor profile `starfixd-serve` allows the one file, read and write, through a new tunable. The file is created with `O_EXCL`, never replaced, and holds 64 hex digits.
- **A key that cannot be made never stops the daemon** (changed 12 Oct 2026, on review, before this slice merged). As first built, a start that needed the key and could not make it failed with a fix, as a keys file that cannot be written does. That was wrong for telemetry: §16's goal is that it never fails a request, and a daemon that will not start fails them all; without the keys file nobody can sign in, but without this key the server works and only the labelling of people in spans is affected; and an operator who had set `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` for metrics as slice O1's guide says would have had a server that refused to start after an upgrade, since that variable now turns traces on too. Now, with `principals: pseudonym`, a key that cannot be read or made for any reason (no directory, a read-only file system, the AppArmor profile, a file others can read, a file that holds no key, no home directory to put the default path under) leaves spans saying nothing about who, exactly as `omit` does. No key is ever made up in memory, since stand-ins that change at each start and that `whois` cannot resolve are worse than none, and the name is never sent in its place. An explicitly set `principals_key` is treated as the default path is. `name` and `omit` need no key and are unaffected.
- **It says so in three places.** The log has it at error level when it is found, as short lines: what happened, the key's file, `cause:`, then each `fix:` on a line of its own, so that `systemctl status`, which cuts a long line, shows them. The status report (`sfx admin status`, `starfixd status`) gains how people appear in spans, beside the clock's lines: by stand-ins, by name, left out by the setting, or `TRACES SAY NOTHING ABOUT WHO` with the cause; in JSON it is `server.traces` (`people`: `pseudonym`, `name`, `omit` or `unavailable`; `cause`, `key_file`, `damaged`), which joined protocol 5 as a result field. And `whois` answers `unavailable`, `this server has no key for stand-ins:` and why, in place of `not_found`, which is kept for a stand-in that is nobody's.
- **A key file that is there and cannot be trusted is its own case.** One that others can read, or that does not hold a key, is not a key that could not be made: someone may have read or changed it. The daemon still starts and still names nobody; the log and the status say exactly that (`others can read it`, `CANNOT BE TRUSTED`), and the fix is to replace the file, which makes earlier stand-ins unlinkable, or, for the mode alone and if nobody can have read it, to tighten it. starfixd does not delete or repair the file itself.
- **It retries, at the reaper's tick.** Every 30 seconds a daemon that wants the key and has none looks again: it reads the file, or makes it. So making the directory where the daemon may write, loading the profile's rule, or repairing the file is all an operator does; one line, `spans name people by stand-ins from now on`, says when it took. A cause that changes is logged again; one that stays is not. A unit's new `ReadWritePaths` still needs a restart, because systemd applies it when the unit starts. A running server thus goes from no stand-ins to stand-ins without a restart, and never the other way: once it has the key it keeps it in memory.
- **Deleting the key file takes effect at the next start,** since the daemon holds the key in memory: stop, delete, start. The new key gives every principal a new stand-in, and `whois` answers that an old one is nobody's.
- **Start refusals are printed a line at a time.** `starfixd` prints an error that stops it as its cause and then each `fix:` on its own line, as the clock's refusal already was, so that the refusal of an `OTEL_*` value it cannot honor is not one long line that `systemctl status` cuts before its fix. A test holds each of those to lines of at most 200 bytes.
- **`whois` tries the key against every principal** of the control store and every server admin the config file names, which is at most the `principals` limit of them and the config file's admins. It is the op `principal.whois`, a read, for server admins only, and joined protocol 5 while no release speaks it (`internal/proto/version.go`). A session's stand-in is not resolvable. A server of one project has no control store and so no `whois`; its operator has `authorized_keys` to read the names from, or `principals: name`.
- **The variables are read by starfix,** as O1's are: the endpoint for traces and its own, the headers, the protocol, the timeout, `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER` (`otlp` or `none`), `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` with its argument (the SDK's six samplers; any other is refused at start), and the four `OTEL_BSP_*` variables of the batching, with the queue held to 65,536. `OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=none` now turns off metrics alone. The resource is made once, so metrics and spans carry one `service.instance.id`.
- **Dropped spans are counted exactly, which the SDK does not do.** Its batching processor drops a span its queue has no room for and keeps the count to itself (it reports it only through an experimental self-observability option, to the process's global meter). So the bound is enforced one step earlier, in a processor of starfix's in front of the SDK's: a span is passed on only while fewer than the queue's size are waiting, which leaves the SDK's queue never full, and one that is not is counted. `starfix.telemetry.dropped{signal="traces"}` is every span lost: at that bound, or in an export that failed. For `signal="metrics"` it still counts failed exports, which lose nothing.
- **The exporter's retries are left on for traces,** unlike metrics: a span that is not exported is gone. They end with the export's timeout. A failed export is counted and logged through a second sampler like O1's, one for each signal, so that one's recovery does not speak for the other's.
- **A stop cuts short what it cannot finish.** The SDK's processor, told to stop with a deadline, returns at the deadline and leaves its last exports running for as long as their own timeout. starfix's processor cancels them when the deadline passes, waits for them to end, and counts their spans as dropped, so a stop leaves no goroutine behind.
- **Log lines.** With traces on, a request's line carries the trace id and the id of the server's own span, sampled or not, in place of the caller's span id that decision 27's build logged; with traces off it is as that build left it. The lines of a handshake and of background work carry the ids of the span they were logged under, which for a line inside the sweep or the keys file's write is that span, in the tick's trace, not the tick's root. Nothing of O3 is built: the ids are added to the lines there were.
- **Trace context for each tool call.** The MCP Go SDK (v1.8.0) hands a request's `_meta` to a tool's handler as a map and knows nothing of trace context. The MCP specification reserves `traceparent`, `tracestate` and `baggage` in `_meta` for it from its 2026-07-28 version, after OpenTelemetry's convention for MCP; before that it was the convention alone. `sfx mcp` reads the first two at each call, checks them as a request frame is checked, and puts the context on the Go context of the call (`client.WithTrace`), which the connection sends in place of its own. A call that brings none, or a malformed one, sends the environment's; the renewals between calls always do. `baggage` is not read. No tool or schema changed, and the wire did not: the context rides `tp` and `ts`.
- **What a request costs.** Starting and ending a write's three spans takes 14 ns and allocates nothing with traces off, 0.6 µs and 16 allocations when the trace is not sampled, and 2.0 µs and 30 allocations when it is (Apple M4 Max, Go 1.27.2). Through a server and a real store, a read took 0.35 to 0.54 ms and a write 5.2 to 6.1 ms in all three modes: the spans are below what Dolt's own variation hides, and show only as allocations, 107, 123 and 155 for a read.
- **How many spans.** One for a read; three for a write, and two more for each retry; three for a handshake, twelve when it opens the store; one for a Dolt commit; and for the reaper's tick four on a server of one project, or five and four for each open store. The default of §16.4, to follow the parent and otherwise sample everything, is kept: at a tracker's traffic it is tens of spans a second. An idle server with 100 stores open sends about 400 spans every 30 seconds for the reaper alone, which is what `parentbased_traceidratio` is for, and the guide says so.
- **Under systemd and AppArmor.** Run on the Ubuntu 24.04 virtual machine of the notes above (arm64, systemd 255, AppArmor 4.0.1, the three profiles enforced), with a Python receiver on the machine, and with slice O4's config (`dolt_metrics_url`) in place. With the guide's one variable and nothing else the daemon starts under `starfixd-serve (enforce)`, serves, and posts spans with no stand-in and no name; the log's error lines, the status report and `whois` give the cause, `it cannot be made: no such file or directory`. With the directory made and no restart, the next tick's cause is `read-only file system`. With the unit's `ReadWritePaths` and a restart, under main's profile: `permission denied`, and `DENIED … operation="mknod"` for the file in the kernel log, which is what the profile's new rule is for. With this slice's profile loaded and no restart, the next tick made the key (mode 0600) and logged that stand-ins began; spans then held one, and `whois` resolved it for a server admin. With the file made mode 0644 and a restart: no stand-in, `CANNOT BE TRUSTED` in the status; after `chmod 600` and no restart, the same stand-in again. The 76 checks of §15's run passed with traces on, with no denial and nothing dropped. The first build of this slice, which refused to start without the key, was run on the same machine earlier the same day: a restart kept the key; with the file deleted the next start made another, the same person had another stand-in and the old one resolved to nobody; another user could not read the key, nor could the `starfix` user under the bridge's or the operator's profile; and one tick of the reaper, with one store open and its first prune, was eleven spans. This slice's first commit says the profile's rule was not run under AppArmor; it has been, as this says.
- **Dolt's metrics are read without a span** (after the merge with slice O4, 12 Oct 2026). O4's reader (`internal/server/doltwatch.go`) fetches Dolt's metrics endpoint every 15 seconds for the status report. It is left out of the traces on purpose: it touches no store and serves no request, its failures are already logged and shown in the status, and a span for each read would add two to the four spans an idle server of one project sends every 30 seconds for nothing an operator would look up in a trace. No span name was added for it, and nothing of Dolt's answer reaches a span.
- **A real Collector.** An OpenTelemetry Collector (0.162.0, in Docker, on the developer's machine) received and decoded the spans and metrics of a daemon started as `serve` starts it: the handshake's, a request's under the trace id of the `traceparent` it was sent, the attempt and the wait beneath it, with the attributes above and no title, name or `tracestate`. That was one run by hand; the tests use a receiver of their own, and the harness of §16.7 is not built.
- **The soak, with a dead collector.** The short soak, and one of two minutes (23,815 requests), were run by hand with metrics and traces both sent to a closed port: every invariant held, the requests were no slower (248 a second against 223 with telemetry off, on the same seed), and the log had one warning for each signal. The soak does not do this by itself yet; that is O5's.
- **Still not run:** amd64; a hosted backend; traces over HTTPS or to a collector by a name that needs DNS, which the profile's three optional lines are for and which were run for metrics only.
- **Left for the later slices.** O3: the ids reach a line through a logger made for it at each place (`withIDs`), not through the log's handler, which the schema will want to do once for every line; `req` is not built, and the request's span id is the nearest thing to it today; `telemetry.principals` governs spans only, and exported log records are to follow it. O5: the smoke test's check that a trace arrives whose root is the test's `TRACEPARENT`; the soak's cross-check of spans against the event log; and a dashboard and an alert on `starfix.telemetry.dropped{signal="traces"}`.

**As built (the status report does not wait for Dolt, 12 Oct 2026).** With Dolt stopped by `SIGSTOP`, `starfixd status` waited its 30 seconds and failed with a read timeout: the report counted each open store's sessions one store after another, each within the request's 60 seconds. The counts now run at once under one deadline of 2 seconds, the bound of `/readyz`'s own check of Dolt, so the report takes that long at most with all 64 stores open; a count that does not come back is `sessions_unknown` in the result, with the cause as text, and `unknown` in the table with a line that says why, never 0. It is a result field, so the protocol is unchanged. Nothing else on the report's path reads Dolt or holds a lock across a read of it: the control store's lists and counts, the registry, the clock, the keys file and Dolt's own figures are read from memory. Measured with Dolt frozen: 30.0 s and an error before, 2.0 s and the report after. **Left as it is:** a new connection still waits on Dolt before its first request is read, to note the session in the registry (up to the request timeout, 62 s measured for `status` on a new connection to an open store) or to open the project's store (60 s, then `unavailable`), so `sfx admin status`, which connects for each command, is prompt only where a connection is already open; `starfixd status` is the command for a server whose Dolt hangs. The local status connection starts no span, as before.

**As built (a new connection does not wait for Dolt before its first request, 12 Oct 2026).** What the note above left as it was is changed, since `sfx` connects for each command and `sfx admin status` is what an admin runs from their own machine when the database is in trouble. (1) **Store open.** The note of the session in the project's registry is made beside the read of the first request, not before it. Every request waits for it, for as long as the request may wait at all, so no request reads or writes a store for a session the registry has not been told of, and a failed note is logged and does not fail the request, as before. For every op but `status` that is the request's timeout. `status` counts its asker's session, so it waits too, but its 2 seconds on Dolt now cover the wait for the note and its own reads together: with Dolt frozen it answers in 2 seconds with the sessions unknown, and otherwise it counts its asker as before. A request that finds the note failed makes it again, within its own time, so a session is noted by its first request that Dolt answers; before, it stayed out of `sfx who` until it renewed a claim. The handler waits for the note before it returns, so the goroutine never outlives the connection or its hold on the store: a connection that closes with its note still being made, which only one that asked nothing can, gives it 2 seconds to finish, so that a session which said hello and left is still listed when Dolt answers, and then cancels it. The `handshake` span now ends at the welcome; the note's write is beneath it in the same trace and may end after it. (2) **Store closed.** A connection is to a project, and its project's store is opened in the handshake; the report opens no store. Rather than change how a connection binds to a project, the handshake gives Dolt 2 seconds to answer a ping of the control database before it opens a store, and refuses with `unavailable` when it does not: `the server's database does not answer, so project "…" was not opened`, with `starfixd status` on the server as the fix, and `dolt.check_failed` in the log. The ping is the handshake's own, not the one `/readyz` caches for 5 seconds, and is made only when a store would be opened. Not done: welcoming such a connection with no store, to answer `status` alone, or opening the store at the first request that needs it; either makes a session without a project, which the connection limits, the tracking of whose connection is whose and every admin op assume it has. (3) **Any other request** on a new connection is refused with `the server timed out on …` after one request timeout, where it was two (the note, then the request). No protocol change. Measured with Dolt frozen, the last with a request timeout of 4 s: `status` on a new connection to an open store 62 s before and 2.0 s after; to a closed store, 60 s and `the server could not open project` before, 2.0 s and the refusal above after; `list` on a new connection 8.0 s before and 4.0 s after.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O3: the log schema, `event`, `req`, the audit stream and OTLP logs, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O3 of §16.8 is built: every line starfixd logs names an event from one list and has only fields from one list; a request's id is on every line logged for it and in its refusal; the ids reach a line through the log's handler; the control store's access changes are also log lines with `audit=true`; and with `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp` the lines also go out over OTLP, under `telemetry.principals`. `telemetry.audit_log` is a setting now. The harness (O5) is not built. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **What it cost.** Two modules: `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/sdk/log` v1.47.0, which is the Logs SDK and is at the SDK's own version, and `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/exporters/otlp/otlplog/otlploghttp` v0.23.0, the exporter §16.2 calls experimental and which is still below version 1. `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/log`, the Logs API, was already linked through the SDK and is now named in `go.mod`. No other module's version changed. With the release flags, and against main after slice O2, `starfixd` went from 21,414,048 to 21,852,320 bytes for linux/amd64 (2.0% more) and from 20,439,602 to 20,882,402 for darwin/arm64, and from 37 third-party modules linked to 39. `sfx` links the same 12 and none of the SDK; it is the same size to the byte on linux/amd64 and 16 bytes larger on darwin/arm64, for the one line it prints (below).
- **No `slog` bridge.** §16.4 says the records go out "through the `slog` bridge", which is `go.opentelemetry.io/contrib/bridges/otelslog`. It is not used: it copies every attribute of a record, and what a record may carry has to be decided field by field (below), which is forty lines over the Logs API. That is one module fewer than §16.2's table counted.
- **The lists are a package, `internal/logs`,** on the standard library alone, so that the store can import it: the fields (`schema.go`), the events (`events.go`), the request's id and the handler. A test parses every log call in `cmd/starfixd`, `internal/server`, `internal/store`, `internal/telemetry`, `internal/sdnotify` and `internal/doltmetrics` and fails unless its message is a string literal, its first field is `"event"` with a constant of the list, and every field it names is in the schema; a second holds `docs/server.md` to both lists. A call that passes a slice of fields is checked by the tests that read the lines themselves: a run with marked text in every argument of every op, whose every line must name a listed event and have only listed fields.
- **77 events, 86 fields.** §16.4's three examples are in the list as it gives them (`keys_file.write_failed`, `project.open_failed`, `conn.cap_refused`). Every line has an event, not only those "worth an alert": a line without one would be the one that turns out to matter. A request's line is `request.done` at debug or `request.refused` at info; the server's own failure is `request.failed`.
- **Fields that changed their names.** `ms` on a request's line is `dur_ms`, as §16.4 has it. Where a line named a principal who is not the line's own, the field is now `subject` (a key held back from the keys file, the lines about a new member's keys), so that `principal` always means whose connection, request or claim it is. `key` is `fingerprint` (a configured admin's key) or `path` (the stand-in key's file); the sampler's `key` is `principal`; `by` on the clock's step line is `step`.
- **The lines about the stand-in key are one event with fields,** not messages built from the cause and the fix: `msg=cause cause="…"`, `msg=fix fix="…"`, a line each as before. A message has to be fixed text for the rule about exported records to mean anything.
- **`req` is 16 hex digits, chosen at random by the server for each request.** It is on the context, so every line logged with the request's context has it: the request's own, the store's failure, a part of the answer that could not be read. It is `req` on the refusal (`proto.Error`), a result field, so there is no protocol change: an older client ignores it and an older server sends none. `sfx` prints it as a third line, `req:`, only when the server itself failed the request (`unavailable`), since any other refusal says what to do and needs no log; `--json` carries it whenever it is there; and `sfx mcp` gives it to the agent to pass on, for `unavailable` only and only when it is 16 hex digits, since it is the server's text in what the agent is told. A line outside a request has none.
- **The ids come from the handler** (`logs.Handler`), once: it reads the request's id and the span's ids from the context of the call and adds them after the line's own fields. `withIDs` and the request line's own reading of its span are gone; a call site logs with its context (`log.ErrorContext(ctx, …)`) and names neither. With traces off the ids are still the caller's, as decision 27's build had them. The schema is flat: a group opened on the logger qualifies the keys that follow and nests nothing.
- **The audit stream is read from the event log,** not written beside it. After each `sfx admin` write is answered for, and at each tick of the reaper, the daemon reads the control store's events past the last it wrote a line for and writes one for each that changes access, in the log's order. So a line cannot say what the log does not, a change made by `starfixd control init` beside the daemon has its line within a tick, and the one thing the daemon keeps is a number in memory. It starts at the newest event there is when the daemon starts: a restart repeats nothing, and what was written while no daemon ran has no line. Keeping the position across restarts would have needed a file or a row of its own, a second thing to be wrong; the event log is there for whoever needs every event.
- **An audit line** has `event` (`audit.` and the event's op, ten of them), `audit=true`, `seq` and `at` (the event's own), `principal` and `session` (the actor), `op`, `target` as the event log names it, and the target's parts where it has them: `project`, `subject`, `fingerprint`, `role`. A key's event holds the key's text in its before and after; a line takes only the principal and the role from those. It carries no `req`: the read after one request can find another's event, and a line that named the wrong request would be worse than one that named none; `seq` finds the event. An event with no actor, and `clock.step`, are not audit lines.
- **`audit_log: false`** writes none. It is in `telemetry:` as §16.3 has it, though the lines are on stderr and need no telemetry.
- **What an exported record carries is decided in `internal/telemetry`, by each field's kind in `internal/logs`,** as a span's attributes are: the message as the body, the level, the event as the record's event name, and the fields that are ids, codes, numbers, times or fixed words, each only in its own shape (a project that is not a UUID is left out, an op the server does not know is `unknown`). `trace_id` and `span_id` are the record's own, from the span on the context. **An `err` is never exported, nor any other text**, under any setting: an error's message quotes what it refused and can name a person, and "exactly as spans do" leaves no room for it. §16.4's "the same records" is therefore the same lines with fewer fields; the journal has the rest, found by `req`.
- **`telemetry.principals` governs every field that names a person,** not only `principal` and `session`: `by`, `subject`, `admin`, `created_by` and `added_by` too. They are the span's stand-ins, from the same key, so exporting logs alone makes the key, and without the key a record says nothing about who and never a name. So an exported audit record names the actor and the subject by stand-ins by default. §16.4 says the setting "does not change the journal or the audit stream": that holds for the stream on stderr, and the copy that leaves the machine follows the setting, which is the reading that never sends a name the operator did not ask to send.
- **Off unless asked for.** `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp` turns it on; with it unset, an endpoint exports metrics and traces and no logs, unlike those two signals, which an endpoint alone turns on. `otlp` with no endpoint is refused at start with a fix, as is any other value. The variables are read by starfix as the others are: the endpoint, headers, protocol and timeout for logs, and the four `OTEL_BLRP_*` of the batching, with the queue held to 65,536.
- **Dropped records are counted exactly,** as spans are and for the same reason: the SDK's batching processor overwrites the oldest record of a full queue and tells only its own logger. A processor of starfix's in front of it passes a record on only while fewer than the queue's size wait, and counts the rest on `starfix.telemetry.dropped{signal="logs"}`, with the records of an export that failed. A stop cuts short what it cannot finish and counts it.
- **The line that says a log export failed is itself exported.** It cannot feed itself: it is sampled, once and then every ten minutes. The lines of the stop are not exported: the export is taken off the logger before telemetry stops.
- **What a line costs.** A line that is exported takes 2.7 µs and 32 allocations more on the goroutine that logged it; with logs not exported the handler adds nothing that allocates (Apple M4 Max, Go 1.27.2). The requests of the dead-collector test were no slower with every line, at debug, exported to a closed port or to a listener that never answers, and the log had one warning.
- **The driver's logger.** `go-sql-driver/mysql` logs through each store's logger now (`dolt.driver`, at warn, with what it said in `detail`), by the connector's own setting, not the package's global one, so two stores do not share it. A test restarts Dolt under an open store and reads the line.
- **Dolt's metrics.** The reader's lines are `dolt_metrics.reading`, `dolt_metrics.read_failed` and `dolt_metrics.read_recovered`.
